I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.

But I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.

Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.

It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.

But since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.

I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.

So I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / meeting_convention_or_event_planner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking my face in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird, and it can eat up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.

Before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even if I’m pretty sure they’re going to say no.

It’s embarrassing to admit, because I know it can seem excessive. But it does take up time sometimes—enough that I fall behind on planning things I’m supposed to be doing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually noticing this uneven part of your face, or is it more that you're worried about what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.\n\nBefore I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I think I could stop for a little bit if I really forced myself, maybe. But it feels really hard in the moment.

Like before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check once, and then I end up opening my phone camera again, retaking the photo, tilting my head different ways… or asking someone at work if I look weird. I know that probably sounds excessive, sorry.

It’s not like I want to be doing it. It just feels like I can’t quite let it go, and then sometimes it eats up enough time that I’m behind on the planning stuff I’m supposed to be doing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually noticing this uneven part of your face, or is it more that you're worried about what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.\n\nBefore I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. But it does take up time sometimes—enough that I fall behind on planning things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your face if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think I could stop for a little bit if I really forced myself, maybe. But it feels really hard in the moment.\n\nLike before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check once, and then I end up opening my phone camera again, retaking the photo, tilting my head different ways… or asking someone at work if I look weird. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I think it’s more the specific part, honestly. Like, I don’t know if “fixing” is the right word, but I get stuck on this one area of my face that looks uneven to me in photos.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually noticing this uneven part of your face, or is it more that you're worried about what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.\n\nBefore I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even if I’m pretty sure they’re going to say no.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, because I know it can seem excessive. But it does take up time sometimes—enough that I fall behind on planning things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your face if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think I could stop for a little bit if I really forced myself, maybe. But it feels really hard in the moment.\n\nLike before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check once, and then I end up opening my phone camera again, retaking the photo, tilting my head different ways… or asking someone at work if I look weird. I know that probably sounds excessive, sorry.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing it. It just feels like I can’t quite let it go, and then sometimes it eats up enough time that I’m behind on the planning stuff I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check your face more about making sure it looks symmetrical or more about fixing the specific part of your face that you think looks uneven?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s more the specific part, honestly. Like, I don’t know if “fixing” is the right word, but I get stuck on this one area of my face that looks uneven to me in photos.\n\nIt got worse once I had to be more in front of people at work, like in event pictures or things like that. Other people say they barely notice anything, so I know maybe I’m making it into too much, but I still end up staring at it and trying to make it look… less uneven, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're checking your face because of how it looks or because you feel like you have to check it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / meeting_convention_or_event_planner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking my face in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird, and it can eat up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it starts because of how it looks… like that uneven part just catches my attention and I feel like I need to make sure it isn’t showing too much.

But then it turns into this feeling like I have to check, if that makes sense. Like before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll just look in my phone camera “one more time,” or retake it, or tilt my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, which I know is probably annoying, or unnecessary.

It’s not constant all day or anything, but it can take longer than I mean for it to, and then I’m behind on planning stuff. Does that answer what you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually noticing this uneven part of your face, or is it more that you're worried about what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.\n\nBefore I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even if I’m pretty sure they’re going to say no.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, because I know it can seem excessive. But it does take up time sometimes—enough that I fall behind on planning things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your face if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think I could stop for a little bit if I really forced myself, maybe. But it feels really hard in the moment.\n\nLike before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check once, and then I end up opening my phone camera again, retaking the photo, tilting my head different ways… or asking someone at work if I look weird. I know that probably sounds excessive, sorry.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing it. It just feels like I can’t quite let it go, and then sometimes it eats up enough time that I’m behind on the planning stuff I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check your face more about making sure it looks symmetrical or more about fixing the specific part of your face that you think looks uneven?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s more the specific part, honestly. Like, I don’t know if “fixing” is the right word, but I get stuck on this one area of my face that looks uneven to me in photos.\n\nIt got worse once I had to be more in front of people at work, like in event pictures or things like that. Other people say they barely notice anything, so I know maybe I’m making it into too much, but I still end up staring at it and trying to make it look… less uneven, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because of how it looks or because you feel like you have to check it?\n\nPatient: I think it starts because of how it looks… like that uneven part just catches my attention and I feel like I need to make sure it isn’t showing too much.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling like I have to check, if that makes sense. It just feels like I can’t quite let it go, and then sometimes it eats up enough time that I’m behind on the planning stuff I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check your face more about making sure it looks symmetrical or more about fixing the specific part of your face that you think looks uneven?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s more the specific part, honestly. Like, I don’t know if “fixing” is the right word, but I get stuck on this one area of my face that looks uneven to me in photos.\n\nIt got worse once I had to be more in front of people at work, like in event pictures or things like that. Other people say they barely notice anything, so I know maybe I’m making it into too much, but I still end up staring at it and trying to make it look… less uneven, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because of how it looks or because you feel like you have to check it?\n\nPatient: I think it starts because of how it looks… like that uneven part just catches my attention and I feel like I need to make sure it isn’t showing too much.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling like I have to check, if that makes sense. Like before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll just look in my phone camera “one more time,” or retake it, or tilt my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, which I know is probably annoying, or unnecessary.\n\nIt’s not constant all day or anything, but it can take longer than I mean for it to, and then I’m behind on planning stuff. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're checking your face to make it look a certain way, or because you feel compelled to do so?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / meeting_convention_or_event_planner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking my face in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way, or ask a coworker if I look weird, and it can eat up enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… probably both, but more like I feel compelled once it starts.

At first I’m trying to make it look a certain way, like if I’m sending event pictures or about to join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in the phone camera and try to tilt my head so that uneven part doesn’t show as much. But then it’s like I can’t just leave it there. I’ll retake photos, check again, or sometimes ask a coworker if I look weird.

I know that sounds like a lot. I’m not trying to be difficult or vain, it just kind of grabs me, and then I realize I’ve spent too much time on it and I’m behind on the planning stuff I was supposed to be doing.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially now that I’m more in front of people for work. There’s this one part of my face that looks uneven to me in photos, and I keep staring at it even though other people say they can barely notice anything. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it’s been hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do check more than I probably should. Like before I send out event pictures or hop on a vendor meeting, I’ll look at my face in my phone camera a bunch, or retake the photo until it looks less… off.\n\nSometimes I tilt my head a certain way so it doesn’t show as much, and I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even though I know that’s probably annoying. It can take longer than I want to admit, and then I’m behind on actual planning stuff. I know that sounds kind of excessive.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything, but it has started getting in the way more than I want to admit. I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I spent so much time trying to deal with how I looked in the event materials, like checking and rechecking pictures and stuff.\n\nAnd now vendor calls and meeting prep take me longer than they should, because I get caught up making sure my face looks okay first. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but… it does affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, I don’t think… it’s not like I have to do it five times or something. It’s more like I keep doing it until it looks “okay” enough, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before sending event pictures or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera, retake the photo, tilt my head a certain way… and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I know that probably sounds kind of unnecessary. It’s just hard to stop until I feel a little less uncomfortable, and then suddenly I’ve spent way too much time on it and I’m behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about how your face looks to others, or is it more about how it feels to you, like something is wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about how it looks to other people, I think. Like… I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I keep noticing this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nI know other people say they barely see anything, so I feel kind of silly saying it, but once I see it, I can’t really stop staring at it. It just feels like that’s what everyone else must be noticing too, even if they say they’re not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check your face before sending out pictures or going on meetings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think something really bad would happen, exactly… like, I know logically the meeting would still happen or the pictures would still go out.\n\nBut I get this feeling that if I don’t check first, I might miss something really obvious and then everyone will notice how weird my face looks. So before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my phone camera, maybe retake the photo, angle my head differently… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive. It just makes me feel safer, I guess, even though it can take long enough that I end up behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your face needs to look perfect or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say perfect, exactly… I know nobody’s face is perfectly symmetrical. But, um, yeah, I think I feel a lot more comfortable if that uneven part doesn’t stand out.\n\nSince I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just keep noticing it. Even when people tell me they barely see anything, I’ll still stare at it and think, like, “No, it’s there.” I know that probably sounds dramatic, but it’s hard for me to let it go once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually noticing this uneven part of your face, or is it more that you're worried about what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t totally know if they’re actually noticing it. People usually say they barely see anything, so I know maybe it’s more in my head than I want to admit.\n\nBut since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, especially in photos, I just can’t stop staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face. And then I start thinking, if I can see it that clearly, they must be able to see it too… or they might notice it and think I look weird.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vain or silly. It’s just hard for me not to focus on it once I’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you need to do to prevent a specific bad outcome, or is it more about trying to feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about trying to feel better, or like… less uncomfortable with how I look. I’m not really thinking, “If I don’t check, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I feel like I have to make sure my face doesn’t look weird.\n\nSo I’ll check it in my phone camera, retake photos, tilt my head a certain way… sometimes ask a coworker if I look okay. I know that probably sounds like a lot. It’s just supposed to make me feel calmer, but then it can take enough time that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check your face repeatedly even if you knew for certain that it looked fine to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah. I mean, I’d like to say if I knew other people thought I looked fine, I’d just stop, but I don’t think it works that cleanly for me.\n\nBefore I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I still end up checking my face in my phone camera, or retaking the picture, or turning my head a little to see if it looks better. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird?” even if I’m pretty sure they’re going to say no.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to admit, because I know it can seem excessive. But it does take up time sometimes—enough that I fall behind on planning things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your face if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think I could stop for a little bit if I really forced myself, maybe. But it feels really hard in the moment.\n\nLike before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check once, and then I end up opening my phone camera again, retaking the photo, tilting my head different ways… or asking someone at work if I look weird. I know that probably sounds excessive, sorry.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing it. It just feels like I can’t quite let it go, and then sometimes it eats up enough time that I’m behind on the planning stuff I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check your face more about making sure it looks symmetrical or more about fixing the specific part of your face that you think looks uneven?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s more the specific part, honestly. Like, I don’t know if “fixing” is the right word, but I get stuck on this one area of my face that looks uneven to me in photos.\n\nIt got worse once I had to be more in front of people at work, like in event pictures or things like that. Other people say they barely notice anything, so I know maybe I’m making it into too much, but I still end up staring at it and trying to make it look… less uneven, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because of how it looks or because you feel like you have to check it?\n\nPatient: I think it starts because of how it looks… like that uneven part just catches my attention and I feel like I need to make sure it isn’t showing too much.\n\nBut then it turns into this feeling like I have to check, if that makes sense. Like before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll just look in my phone camera “one more time,” or retake it, or tilt my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, which I know is probably annoying, or unnecessary.\n\nIt’s not constant all day or anything, but it can take longer than I mean for it to, and then I’m behind on planning stuff. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face to make it look a certain way, or because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, but more like I feel compelled once it starts.\n\nAt first I’m trying to make it look a certain way, like if I’m sending event pictures or about to join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in the phone camera and try to tilt my head so that uneven part doesn’t show as much. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face to make it look a certain way, or because you feel compelled to do so?\n\nPatient: I think… probably both, but more like I feel compelled once it starts.\n\nAt first I’m trying to make it look a certain way, like if I’m sending event pictures or about to join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in the phone camera and try to tilt my head so that uneven part doesn’t show as much. But then it’s like I can’t just leave it there. I’ll retake photos, check again, or sometimes ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot. I’m not trying to be difficult or vain, it just kind of grabs me, and then I realize I’ve spent too much time on it and I’m behind on the planning stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you would still feel the need to check your face even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

Um… I think if I were completely alone and nobody was going to see me, it probably wouldn’t be as strong. Like, the worst of it is before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, because I’m worried that uneven part is going to show.
